Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.

And it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.

So yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.

The house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.

So yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.

I yelled, and then afterward I was shaking because I felt so awful. That’s not how I usually treat people. I don’t like being that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel irritable even when nothing in particular is annoying you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m already on edge before anything even happens, and then something tiny just sets me off.\n\nLike with the stock thing—I keep coming back to that because it was so stupid, honestly. Wrong shelf, that’s all it was. I’ve worked retail long enough to know that happens constantly, especially when things get busy or someone’s new or distracted. And I just… blew up. I yelled at them like they’d done something terrible.\n\nAnd then afterward I was shaking, not even just mad anymore, more like embarrassed and kind of scared of myself, because that’s not how I usually treat people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel irritable at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? It’s hard to say, because lately it feels like I’m kind of walking around already wound up, even before anything happens. Like I’m not always yelling, obviously, but the irritability is just sitting there.\n\nAnd then something tiny happens—like that stock on the wrong shelf thing—and I just blow up like it’s some huge deal. Which it wasn’t. I know it wasn’t. I keep thinking about that, because afterward I was literally shaking and thinking, what is wrong with me, I don’t treat people like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this level of irritability?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I think. It kind of started after the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve settled down by now, because it wasn’t like… I don’t know, I’ve had stressful things happen before. Moving, repairs, customers yelling at me—retail gives you plenty of practice with that. But since that weekend, it’s like I’ve just been stuck this way. So yeah… about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It kind of started after the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve settled down by now, because it wasn’t like… I don’t know, I’ve had stressful things happen before. Moving, repairs, customers yelling at me—retail gives you plenty of practice with that. But since that weekend, it’s like I’ve just been stuck this way. So yeah… about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. What was the question exactly? Less interested? I don’t know if I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. It’s all kind of blurred together lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire happening?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. That’s a hard one.\n\nI keep going over little pieces of it, like whether I should’ve noticed something sooner, or if I had moved differently— but then I start thinking about how at work we have those safety posters in the break room that nobody reads, and one time the laminator overheated and everyone just joked about it for weeks, which is… not really what you asked.\n\nResponsible? I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I don’t know what part was me and what part was just… what happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. What was the question exactly? Less interested? I don’t know if I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. It’s all kind of blurred together lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire happening?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. That’s a hard one.\n\nI keep going over little pieces of it, like whether I should’ve noticed something sooner, or if I had moved differently— but then I start thinking about how at work we have those safety posters in the break room that nobody reads, and one time the laminator overheated and everyone just joked about it for weeks, which is… not really what you asked.\n\nResponsible? I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I don’t know what part was me and what part was just… what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing fewer of the things you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m having trouble separating what I “normally enjoy” from just things I do out of habit. Like, I used to go look around the garden section after my shift sometimes, not even to buy anything, just to see what new plants came in. But then sometimes I still end up there because it’s right by the exit at work, and I’ll remember I need paper towels or something—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t know if I’m doing fewer things exactly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in a clear way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Loudly.\n\nAnd afterward I was shaking, because that’s not me. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone else, but I don’t usually treat people like that. I just… I don’t feel like I have a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at people or getting angry outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are around me all day, but it feels like it’s not really about the shelf or the stock or whatever. It’s like I just go from zero to furious over nothing.\n\nThat employee putting stuff in the wrong place—I know that happens, I’ve done it myself when we’re busy, and I still just snapped. I yelled, and then afterward I was shaking because I felt so awful. That’s not how I usually treat people. I don’t like being that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel irritable even when nothing in particular is annoying you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m already on edge before anything even happens, and then something tiny just sets me off.\n\nLike with the stock thing—I keep coming back to that because it was so stupid, honestly. Wrong shelf, that’s all it was. I’ve worked retail long enough to know that happens constantly, especially when things get busy or someone’s new or distracted. And I just… blew up. I yelled at them like they’d done something terrible.\n\nAnd then afterward I was shaking, not even just mad anymore, more like embarrassed and kind of scared of myself, because that’s not how I usually treat people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel irritable at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? It’s hard to say, because lately it feels like I’m kind of walking around already wound up, even before anything happens. Like I’m not always yelling, obviously, but the irritability is just sitting there.\n\nAnd then something tiny happens—like that stock on the wrong shelf thing—and I just blow up like it’s some huge deal. Which it wasn’t. I know it wasn’t. I keep thinking about that, because afterward I was literally shaking and thinking, what is wrong with me, I don’t treat people like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this level of irritability?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I think. It kind of started after the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve settled down by now, because it wasn’t like… I don’t know, I’ve had stressful things happen before. Moving, repairs, customers yelling at me—retail gives you plenty of practice with that. But since that weekend, it’s like I’ve just been stuck this way. So yeah… about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. What was the question exactly? Less interested? I don’t know if I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. It’s all kind of blurred together lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire happening?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. That’s a hard one.\n\nI keep going over little pieces of it, like whether I should’ve noticed something sooner, or if I had moved differently— but then I start thinking about how at work we have those safety posters in the break room that nobody reads, and one time the laminator overheated and everyone just joked about it for weeks, which is… not really what you asked.\n\nResponsible? I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I don’t know what part was me and what part was just… what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing fewer of the things you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m having trouble separating what I “normally enjoy” from just things I do out of habit. Like, I used to go look around the garden section after my shift sometimes, not even to buy anything, just to see what new plants came in. But then sometimes I still end up there because it’s right by the exit at work, and I’ll remember I need paper towels or something—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t know if I’m doing fewer things exactly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do regularly before the kitchen fire that you haven't done at all since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there are.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the house. Not really. I keep telling myself I need to, because there are things there and mail and whatever, but then I’ll get in the car and somehow end up doing errands instead. And there’s this mortgage folder I used to keep pretty organized—payments, insurance stuff, all those boring papers—and I haven’t even opened it. It’s just sitting there like it’s… I don’t know, like it’s hot to touch or something.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio, which sounds ridiculous, I know. I used to fuss over them all the time. Basil, thyme, little mint plant that kept trying to take over everything—but now I look at them and it pulls me right back to that day, and my chest gets tight, so I just… don’t go near them. I tell myself I’ll do it later, and then I don’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure “lost interest” is the right way to say it. Like, if something used to feel nice, I don’t know if it stopped being nice or if I just… don’t get myself to the part where I do it.

I don’t think I can say “not at all.” That feels too definite.

Like… I haven’t really tested it, if that makes sense. I haven’t sat down and done something and thought, wow, there’s nothing there. It’s more that I don’t get around to doing it, or I start thinking about other things. Even little stuff, like watching those home renovation shows—I used to put them on while folding laundry, and now I’ll scroll past them and think, do I really want to look at someone’s kitchen? So… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed something where I’m positive I get no pleasure from it anymore.

It’s like I just go from zero to furious over nothing.\n\nThat employee putting stuff in the wrong place—I know that happens, I’ve done it myself when we’re busy, and I still just snapped. I yelled, and then afterward I was shaking because I felt so awful. That’s not how I usually treat people. I don’t like being that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel irritable even when nothing in particular is annoying you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m already on edge before anything even happens, and then something tiny just sets me off.\n\nLike with the stock thing—I keep coming back to that because it was so stupid, honestly. Wrong shelf, that’s all it was. I’ve worked retail long enough to know that happens constantly, especially when things get busy or someone’s new or distracted. And I just… blew up. I yelled at them like they’d done something terrible.\n\nAnd then afterward I was shaking, not even just mad anymore, more like embarrassed and kind of scared of myself, because that’s not how I usually treat people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel irritable at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? It’s hard to say, because lately it feels like I’m kind of walking around already wound up, even before anything happens. Like I’m not always yelling, obviously, but the irritability is just sitting there.\n\nAnd then something tiny happens—like that stock on the wrong shelf thing—and I just blow up like it’s some huge deal. Which it wasn’t. I know it wasn’t. I keep thinking about that, because afterward I was literally shaking and thinking, what is wrong with me, I don’t treat people like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this level of irritability?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I think. It kind of started after the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve settled down by now, because it wasn’t like… I don’t know, I’ve had stressful things happen before. Moving, repairs, customers yelling at me—retail gives you plenty of practice with that. But since that weekend, it’s like I’ve just been stuck this way. So yeah… about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. What was the question exactly? Less interested? I don’t know if I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. It’s all kind of blurred together lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire happening?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. That’s a hard one.\n\nI keep going over little pieces of it, like whether I should’ve noticed something sooner, or if I had moved differently— but then I start thinking about how at work we have those safety posters in the break room that nobody reads, and one time the laminator overheated and everyone just joked about it for weeks, which is… not really what you asked.\n\nResponsible? I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I don’t know what part was me and what part was just… what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing fewer of the things you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m having trouble separating what I “normally enjoy” from just things I do out of habit. Like, I used to go look around the garden section after my shift sometimes, not even to buy anything, just to see what new plants came in. But then sometimes I still end up there because it’s right by the exit at work, and I’ll remember I need paper towels or something—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t know if I’m doing fewer things exactly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do regularly before the kitchen fire that you haven't done at all since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there are.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the house. Not really. I keep telling myself I need to, because there are things there and mail and whatever, but then I’ll get in the car and somehow end up doing errands instead. And there’s this mortgage folder I used to keep pretty organized—payments, insurance stuff, all those boring papers—and I haven’t even opened it. It’s just sitting there like it’s… I don’t know, like it’s hot to touch or something.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio, which sounds ridiculous, I know. I used to fuss over them all the time. Basil, thyme, little mint plant that kept trying to take over everything—but now I look at them and it pulls me right back to that day, and my chest gets tight, so I just… don’t go near them. I tell myself I’ll do it later, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure “lost interest” is the right way to say it. Like, if something used to feel nice, I don’t know if it stopped being nice or if I just… don’t get myself to the part where I do it.\n\nI still think, oh, that would be good, maybe I should do that, and then I get distracted or there’s some reason not to. Like when I’m at work and the seasonal aisle changes over, I used to kind of enjoy seeing all the silly little decorations, and now I’ll notice them and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to clock out correctly or if the register drawer was short that one time last year—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t clearly noticed losing interest, exactly. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to find pleasurable, but now you don't find pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “not at all.” That feels too definite.\n\nLike… I haven’t really tested it, if that makes sense. I haven’t sat down and done something and thought, wow, there’s nothing there. It’s more that I don’t get around to doing it, or I start thinking about other things. Even little stuff, like watching those home renovation shows—I used to put them on while folding laundry, and now I’ll scroll past them and think, do I really want to look at someone’s kitchen? And then I end up watching some stupid clip about organizing drawers or, I don’t know, people making tiny dollhouse furniture.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed something where I’m positive I get no pleasure from it anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… yes. I mean, I want to say I’m still managing, because I do show up sometimes and I keep thinking if I just make a list it’ll be fine, but it’s not really fine.

I missed a vendor meeting at work, which I never do—or I didn’t used to. And then one shift I just… left before I was done. So, yeah. It’s affecting work, and home, and I’m falling behind faster than I can fix it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I, um… I’m here because I keep snapping at people. Like, really blowing up over nothing. I work retail, and the other day one of my employees put stock on the wrong shelf, which happens, you know, it’s not the end of the world—and I just yelled at them. Loudly.\n\nAnd afterward I was shaking, because that’s not me. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone else, but I don’t usually treat people like that. I just… I don’t feel like I have a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at people or getting angry outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are around me all day, but it feels like it’s not really about the shelf or the stock or whatever. It’s like I just go from zero to furious over nothing.\n\nThat employee putting stuff in the wrong place—I know that happens, I’ve done it myself when we’re busy, and I still just snapped. I yelled, and then afterward I was shaking because I felt so awful. That’s not how I usually treat people. I don’t like being that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel irritable even when nothing in particular is annoying you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m already on edge before anything even happens, and then something tiny just sets me off.\n\nLike with the stock thing—I keep coming back to that because it was so stupid, honestly. Wrong shelf, that’s all it was. I’ve worked retail long enough to know that happens constantly, especially when things get busy or someone’s new or distracted. And I just… blew up. I yelled at them like they’d done something terrible.\n\nAnd then afterward I was shaking, not even just mad anymore, more like embarrassed and kind of scared of myself, because that’s not how I usually treat people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel irritable at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? It’s hard to say, because lately it feels like I’m kind of walking around already wound up, even before anything happens. Like I’m not always yelling, obviously, but the irritability is just sitting there.\n\nAnd then something tiny happens—like that stock on the wrong shelf thing—and I just blow up like it’s some huge deal. Which it wasn’t. I know it wasn’t. I keep thinking about that, because afterward I was literally shaking and thinking, what is wrong with me, I don’t treat people like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this level of irritability?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I think. It kind of started after the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve settled down by now, because it wasn’t like… I don’t know, I’ve had stressful things happen before. Moving, repairs, customers yelling at me—retail gives you plenty of practice with that. But since that weekend, it’s like I’ve just been stuck this way. So yeah… about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. What was the question exactly? Less interested? I don’t know if I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. It’s all kind of blurred together lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire happening?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. That’s a hard one.\n\nI keep going over little pieces of it, like whether I should’ve noticed something sooner, or if I had moved differently— but then I start thinking about how at work we have those safety posters in the break room that nobody reads, and one time the laminator overheated and everyone just joked about it for weeks, which is… not really what you asked.\n\nResponsible? I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I don’t know what part was me and what part was just… what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing fewer of the things you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m having trouble separating what I “normally enjoy” from just things I do out of habit. I don’t know if I’m doing fewer things exactly. I don’t know if I’d put it that way. “Worthless” feels like such a big word, you know? Like something people say in a movie when they’re standing in the rain.

Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning that word over and it doesn’t land neatly.

I mean, I think about the fire a lot, but “guilty” feels like it would mean I know exactly what I did wrong and I’m admitting to it, and I don’t even know if that’s the right way to say it. Sometimes I’ll be doing something totally unrelated, like looking for a receipt in my purse, and then I’ll start wondering whether the stove was off, or if I should’ve checked something twice, and then somehow I’m thinking about how my mother used to unplug the toaster every single time, even if she only made one piece of toast. She was very intense about that kind of thing.

So… I guess I’m not sure if it’s guilt or just going in circles about it. It’s hard to separate.

Loudly.\n\nAnd afterward I was shaking, because that’s not me. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone else, but I don’t usually treat people like that. I just… I don’t feel like I have a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at people or getting angry outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are around me all day, but it feels like it’s not really about the shelf or the stock or whatever. It’s like I just go from zero to furious over nothing.\n\nThat employee putting stuff in the wrong place—I know that happens, I’ve done it myself when we’re busy, and I still just snapped. I yelled, and then afterward I was shaking because I felt so awful. That’s not how I usually treat people. I don’t like being that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel irritable even when nothing in particular is annoying you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m already on edge before anything even happens, and then something tiny just sets me off.\n\nLike with the stock thing—I keep coming back to that because it was so stupid, honestly. Wrong shelf, that’s all it was. I’ve worked retail long enough to know that happens constantly, especially when things get busy or someone’s new or distracted. And I just… blew up. I yelled at them like they’d done something terrible.\n\nAnd then afterward I was shaking, not even just mad anymore, more like embarrassed and kind of scared of myself, because that’s not how I usually treat people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you don't feel irritable at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? It’s hard to say, because lately it feels like I’m kind of walking around already wound up, even before anything happens. Like I’m not always yelling, obviously, but the irritability is just sitting there.\n\nAnd then something tiny happens—like that stock on the wrong shelf thing—and I just blow up like it’s some huge deal. Which it wasn’t. I know it wasn’t. I keep thinking about that, because afterward I was literally shaking and thinking, what is wrong with me, I don’t treat people like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this level of irritability?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I think. It kind of started after the kitchen fire, during that first weekend we were working on the house.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve settled down by now, because it wasn’t like… I don’t know, I’ve had stressful things happen before. Moving, repairs, customers yelling at me—retail gives you plenty of practice with that. But since that weekend, it’s like I’ve just been stuck this way. So yeah… about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family, it’s mostly that I’m not keeping up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as relationship problems exactly, but it’s putting strain on everything.\n\nWork has been bad too. I missed a vendor meeting—I just completely couldn’t deal with it—and I walked out before finishing a shift, which I’ve never done. And at home… I can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so all the house stuff is just sitting there, and the longer it sits, the worse I feel about it.\n\nIt’s like everything is piling up faster than I can handle, and then I’m embarrassed, so I avoid talking about it, which probably doesn’t help with people around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, activities, or social situations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. Mostly the house. I keep telling myself I need to go over there and deal with things, but I’ll get as far as thinking about the front door and my chest just tightens up, and then I find some excuse not to.\n\nAnd it’s not only the house, which sounds ridiculous. There’s this mortgage folder with paperwork in it, and I won’t even open it. It’s just sitting there like it’s radioactive. Even the herbs on my patio… because they make me think of that day somehow, and I used to like taking care of them, you know? Basil, rosemary, all that. Now I just look away and go back inside.\n\nSo yeah, anything tied to that day, I’m avoiding it. I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding things or places that remind you of the kitchen fire every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day. I try not to, but it’s like my brain has this little list of things it refuses to go near.\n\nThe house is the big one, obviously. But then there’s the mortgage folder—I know that sounds like such a normal, boring thing, paperwork, like something you’d do with coffee on a Sunday—but I can’t open it. And even the herbs on my patio, which is stupid because they’re just plants, but they make me think of that day and my chest tightens, and I just… I go back inside.\n\nSo yes, every day there’s something tied to the fire that I’m avoiding. I can’t seem to make myself face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having unwanted memories or thoughts about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It just kind of jumps into my head when I’m not even trying to think about it.\n\nLike I’ll pick up a pan, or even open a spreadsheet at work—nothing dramatic, just normal stuff—and suddenly it’s like I’m right back there. I can smell smoke again, and I see the kitchen filling up, and it happens before I can stop it.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at whatever I was doing. It’s… it’s not like I’m choosing to remember it. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, sleep has never been perfect for me—retail hours mess you up anyway, you know? One week you’re closing, then someone calls out and suddenly you’re opening, and it throws everything off.\n\nSince the fire… I guess I haven’t really been keeping track. I know I get tired, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s specifically that or just… everything piling up. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself less interested in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… “enjoy” is a weird word right now, because I still do things if I have to. Like I’ll water the patio plants sometimes, though then I start noticing which ones look half-dead, and I think about how I used to be better at keeping them alive—my basil always went crazy in the summer, actually, I used to give some to a neighbor—\n\nSorry. What was the question exactly? Less interested? I don’t know if I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. It’s all kind of blurred together lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the kitchen fire happening?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. That’s a hard one.\n\nI keep going over little pieces of it, like whether I should’ve noticed something sooner, or if I had moved differently— but then I start thinking about how at work we have those safety posters in the break room that nobody reads, and one time the laminator overheated and everyone just joked about it for weeks, which is… not really what you asked.\n\nResponsible? I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I don’t know what part was me and what part was just… what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself doing fewer of the things you normally enjoy since the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m having trouble separating what I “normally enjoy” from just things I do out of habit. Like, I used to go look around the garden section after my shift sometimes, not even to buy anything, just to see what new plants came in. But then sometimes I still end up there because it’s right by the exit at work, and I’ll remember I need paper towels or something—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t know if I’m doing fewer things exactly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do regularly before the kitchen fire that you haven't done at all since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there are.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the house. Not really. I keep telling myself I need to, because there are things there and mail and whatever, but then I’ll get in the car and somehow end up doing errands instead. And there’s this mortgage folder I used to keep pretty organized—payments, insurance stuff, all those boring papers—and I haven’t even opened it. It’s just sitting there like it’s… I don’t know, like it’s hot to touch or something.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio, which sounds ridiculous, I know. I used to fuss over them all the time. Basil, thyme, little mint plant that kept trying to take over everything—but now I look at them and it pulls me right back to that day, and my chest gets tight, so I just… don’t go near them. I tell myself I’ll do it later, and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure “lost interest” is the right way to say it. Like, if something used to feel nice, I don’t know if it stopped being nice or if I just… don’t get myself to the part where I do it.\n\nI still think, oh, that would be good, maybe I should do that, and then I get distracted or there’s some reason not to. Like when I’m at work and the seasonal aisle changes over, I used to kind of enjoy seeing all the silly little decorations, and now I’ll notice them and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I forgot to clock out correctly or if the register drawer was short that one time last year—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t clearly noticed losing interest, exactly. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to find pleasurable, but now you don't find pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “not at all.” That feels too definite.\n\nLike… I haven’t really tested it, if that makes sense. I haven’t sat down and done something and thought, wow, there’s nothing there. It’s more that I don’t get around to doing it, or I start thinking about other things. Even little stuff, like watching those home renovation shows—I used to put them on while folding laundry, and now I’ll scroll past them and think, do I really want to look at someone’s kitchen? And then I end up watching some stupid clip about organizing drawers or, I don’t know, people making tiny dollhouse furniture.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed something where I’m positive I get no pleasure from it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your irritability or reactions have significantly impacted your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I want to say I’m still managing, because I do show up sometimes and I keep thinking if I just make a list it’ll be fine, but it’s not really fine.\n\nI missed a vendor meeting at work, which I never do—or I didn’t used to. And then one shift I just… left before I was done. I told myself I was going to step outside for a minute, like people do when the air conditioning is too high and you just need normal air, but then I got in my car and went home. And the house stuff is worse. I still can’t make myself go back to the damaged house, so there’s mail and paperwork and all these responsibilities just stacking up.\n\nSo, yeah. It’s affecting work, and home, and I’m falling behind faster than I can fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others since the kitchen fire happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. “Worthless” feels like such a big word, you know? Like something people say in a movie when they’re standing in the rain.\n\nI mean, I do worry that people are tired of hearing about it, or that I’m not handling things the way I’m supposed to. But then I start thinking about all these practical things, like who has the spare key, and whether the insurance person needs another copy of something, and I get stuck on that instead.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been thinking, “I’m a burden,” exactly. It’s more like I don’t know where to put myself with all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about the kitchen fire?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I keep turning that word over and it doesn’t land neatly.\n\nI mean, I think about the fire a lot, but “guilty” feels like it would mean I know exactly what I did wrong and I’m admitting to it, and I don’t even know if that’s the right way to say it. Sometimes I’ll be doing something totally unrelated, like looking for a receipt in my purse, and then I’ll start wondering whether the stove was off, or if I should’ve checked something twice, and then somehow I’m thinking about how my mother used to unplug the toaster every single time, even if she only made one piece of toast. She was very intense about that kind of thing.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure if it’s guilt or just going in circles about it. It’s hard to separate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
